南亞科(2408) 
買進, TP 710 (潛在報酬 45.49%) 
買進 
Price(10/13):488 
Target Price：710

展望未來，南亞科(2408.TT) 因受限於極低庫存，2027 年上半年
位元出貨量預期將保持平穩，待 4Q27 5A 新廠開出產能後，出貨
量將恢復逐步成長；產能方面，5A 新廠規劃於 2027 年第一季開
始進場裝機，第一階段預計 2028 年底前投片量達 3 萬片，全產
能 5 萬片目標於 2030 年底達成，且擴建嚴格以客戶 LTA 的 AI
專案為導向；此外，公司規劃於屏東科學園區建置3D 晶圓封裝與
測試廠，預計 2028 下半年導入設備，並與客戶合作開發客製化  
AI/WoW(晶圓堆疊)與 ASIC 專案，已開始產生部分營收貢獻，長
期目標於 2029 至 2030 年將 AI 基礎設施營收占比拉高至 40%
至 50%；技術面上，次世代 1C 製程預計於 2027 年第一季進行
客戶驗證 。 綜合以上 ， 研究部預估南亞科2027 年 EPS 為 130.72
元。考量目前公司記憶體供給吃緊、需求擴大，有長期合約支持未
來成長 ，且公司具備獨特製程升級與客製化產品線成長潛力 ， 研究
部給予 5.5 倍本益比評價，目標價 710 元，投資建議為買進。

⚫ 公司基本資料 南亞科(2408.TT)公司簡介 
南亞科技(2408.TT)成立於 1995 年，隸屬於台塑集團，為全球領先的動
態隨機存取記憶體(DRAM)製造大廠 。 公司生產基地主要位於台灣 ， 擁有
多樣化的產品組合，涵蓋 DDR5、LPDDR5/5X、DDR4、LPDDR4/4X、
DDR3 及 LPDDR3 等產品線 ， 產品種類達數十顆 ， 服務全球數百家客戶 。
面對傳統記憶體產業之劇烈循環波動，公司近年積極推動技術升級與結
構性轉型，除中長期積極投入 1B(16 奈米)與 1C(12 奈米)先進製程轉換
及新五廠建置，並深入布局企業級 SSD(eSSD)、SmartNIC、BMC 等
AI 伺服器基礎設施，同時與客戶合作開發客製化 AI/WoW(晶圓堆疊)利
基產品，正逐步由傳統大宗商品化DRAM 廠，轉型為具備高附加價值與
高敏捷度的全方位利基型記憶體解決方案供應商 。

2Q26 營運表現全面成長，售價調漲驅動獲利大幅提升 
南 亞 科 (2408.TT) 2Q26 營 業 收 入 為 825.49 億元 (Q+68.17% / 
Y+684.23%) ， 營收展現強勁成長 ， 受惠於平均銷售單價(ASP)季增超過
60%的漲勢，有效抵消了位元出貨量與上季持平的量能限制；而在 ASP 
大幅上漲且成本結構維持穩定的推升下，單季毛利率提升至
79.49%(Q+11.60ppt / Y+100.05ppt) 。雖然員工分紅與銷售研發使營業

3Q26 獲利大幅跳升，低價長約重簽驅動 ASP 季增逾六成 
南亞科(2408.TT) 3Q26 營收 1,336.49 億元(Q+61.9% / Y+611.7%)，雖
然出貨量因公司整體庫存已降至極低水位，呈中個位數季減，但整體營
收受惠於平均銷售單價(ASP)季增超過 60%(年增超過 500%)的強勁漲
勢；ASP 強勢上揚的核心主因，在於早期簽訂的低價長期合約 (LTA)於
第三季集中到期並重新議價(對 ASP 季增貢獻度超過半數)。在 ASP 大
幅 上 漲 且 生 產 成 本 結 構 穩 定 的 推 升 下 ， 單 季 毛 利 率 跳 升 至  
85.7%(Q+6.2ppt / Y+67.2ppt) 。費用方面，因增發員工薪酬獎勵，研發
費用增加至 51.45 億元、銷管費用增加至 24.43 億元，合計營業費用
為 75.88 億元 。 綜合以上 ， 南亞科(2408.TT) 3Q26 營業收入為 1,336.49
億元， Q+61.90%/Y+611.70% 、 毛 利 率  85.70% ，
Q+6.21ppt/Y+67.25ppt、 營業利益率 79.98%，Q+6.30ppt/Y+74.02ppt、
稅後純益 876.27 億元，Q+74.58%/Y+虧轉盈、EPS 為 25.40 元。

2026 年受惠 DRAM 報價持續上漲與 AI 伺服器需求，獲利創歷史新高 
展望 2026 年全年，南亞科(2408.TT) 前三季累計淨利已高達 1,639 億
元，累計EPS 達 49.28 元，公司維持 2026 全年位元銷售量成長高十
位數百分比的目標；合約與定價機制上，公司目前長約(LTA)覆蓋率落在 
50% 至 70% 之間，其中 3 年期以上長約占總出貨量 20% 以上，履
約率高達 100%，預期第四季 DRAM 供需緊缺狀態延續，合約平均售
價保持溫和成長；需求端方面，GPU、TPU、CPU 及 ASIC 等 AI 基
礎建設建置強勁，推升 AI 伺服器與基礎設施相關產品之營收占比突破 
20%； 產品組合中 DDR4 與 LPDDR4 合計營收占比超過 60%，DDR5 
占比約 10%。管理層指引目前產業短期內難以緩解供給壓力，隨著第四
季 DRAM ASP 持續看漲 ， 公司毛利率表現有機會再進一步拉升 。 綜合以
上，研究部預估南亞科(2408.TT) 2026 年營業收入為 4,190.95 億元，
Y+529.40%、毛利率  82.61%，Y+60.10ppt、營業利益率  76.83%，
Y+68.96ppt、稅後純益 2,642.16 億元，Y+3,894.93%、EPS 為 76.58
元。

長鑫存儲(CXMT)技術進展與產業影響 
長鑫存儲(CXMT)於 2026 年 9 月宣佈第五代 DRAM 制程(G5)進入量產階
段。儘管目前 G5 產品仍處於客戶驗證與良率提升階段，距離大規模商
業化出貨尚需時間 ， 但已顯示中國DRAM 技術發展速度明顯超出市場原
先預期。 長鑫 HBM3E 良率已達 40%左右。市場普遍認為，長鑫在無法
取得 EUV 設備的情況下，主要透過 ArF DUV 浸潤式曝光搭配多重曝光
(Multiple Patterning) 技術推進制程微縮，因此雖然能夠達到接近國際大
廠先進 DRAM 節點的設計能力 ， 但製造成本 、 良率與生產效率仍與三星 、
SK 海力士及美光存在差距。未來能否持續提升至國際大廠 70%以上的
量產水準，將是觀察長鑫HBM 競爭力的重要指標。短期而言，長鑫技術
進步對台廠商影響有限，長鑫目前仍面臨良率提升與客戶驗證所需時
間，短期內難以快速增加有效供給，因此全球 DRAM 供應緊張的局面仍
將延續至 2027 至 2028 年。相較之下，三星、SK 海力士與美光持續將
產能轉向 HBM 產品，反而造成 DDR4 供給逐漸退出市場，進一步支撐
DDR4 價格維持強勢，有利於以 DDR4 產品為主的南亞科營運表現；然
而，中長期則需注意，長鑫 DRAM 投片量將於 2027 年底達到約 30K 
WPM，並持續擴產，若未來 G5 良率持續改善、HBM 技術逐步成熟，
加上中國市場本土化政策支持，長鑫未來在中低階 DDR5、PC DRAM
及中國本土市場的競爭力可能快速提升 ， 進而對全球DRAM 供需格局產
生實質影響。

長鑫存儲(CXMT)4F²技術 
長鑫存儲在 10 月 12 日有表示年底推出 4F²架構 DDR5 伺服器記憶體，
4F²是將每個記憶體儲存單元配置在面積相當於 4 倍 F 平方的區域內 ， 其
中 F 代表半導體制程的最小線寬，這種設計能讓同一顆晶片容納更多儲
存單元，進而提高記憶體容量。目前，主流商用 DRAM 仍採用 6F 平方
儲存單元結構。長鑫存儲表示，其4F 平方設計的核心，在於自主研發的
垂直環繞閘極(Vertical Surrounding Gate，VSG)電晶體，這項技術透過
垂直閘極架構改善漏電問題，同時大幅縮小單元面積，提高儲存密度。
SK 海力士(SK Hynix) 已於 2025 年公佈 4F 平方技術發展藍圖，另外，
根據南韓媒體今年 4 月的報導，三星電子(Samsung Electronics)計畫於
2028 年前，量產採用這項新設計、制程低於 10 奈米的 DRAM。

成長；產能方面，5A 新廠規劃於 2027 年第一季開始進場裝機，第一

Improving 3Q GPM helped by better mix — Dynapack's preliminary 3Q26 results 
showed margin recovery yet weak PC sales remained the key drag on the earnings. 
3Q26 EPS of NT$2.33 (+72% q/q and +49% y/y) missed our/BBG est by 45%/27%, 
although we believe the earnings shortfall was well anticipated given the 3Q sales 
miss owing to the delayed shipment of one of the BBU products and weaker PC 
demand. GPM improved q/q on a richer product mix, with higher-margin BBU 
making up c.50% of 3Q sales on our estimate, while OPM recovered to 10.7%, 
+4.2ppt q/q and +1.3ppt y/y. Nevertheless, the sales shortfall constrained op 
leverage, leaving margin improvement insufficient to offset weaker sales. 
PSU vendor shortages weigh on Oct; 12kW ramp to drive Nov sales rebound — 
Our industry checks suggest that Dynapack's major PSU vendor has encountered 
component supply shortages (related to power management IC) and therefore 
delayed BBU pull-ins, weighing on shipments of Dynapack's existing 3kW and 5kW 
BBU products in Oct. We therefore expect Oct-26 BBU sales to decline slightly m/m. 
Meanwhile, PC sales should recover sequentially and more than to offset the BBU 
softness, supporting a slight m/m increase in group sales. Looking beyond Oct-26, 
we expect accelerating shipments of the new 12kW BBU series from Nov-26 to drive 
a strong rebound in BBU sales.

Implications — We reiterate our bullish stance on the BBU sector despite near-term
volatility from external supply-chain constraints, which is likely to put pressure on
near-term sentiment. We view the component-driven delays in product pull-ins
primarily as a shipment-timing issue, with underlying demand remaining strong into
2027E. We also see ample opportunity for Dynapack to gain market share from

Panasonic, its major Japanese peer, particularly as power architectures migrate
toward HVDC. This share-gain potential remains intact, reinforcing our confidence
in Dynapack's medium term BBU growth outlook.

Dynapack International (3211.TWO)

Dynapack International
Valuation
We assign a 32x target P/E to our 2027E EPS of NT$25 to derive a target price of NT$800. We value Dynapack based on 2027E
target P/E as we believe 2027E better reflects its earnings power after BBU capacity expansion. Our target P/E of 32x is based
on our forecast EPS CAGR of 66% in 2025-2027E and a PEG of 0.5x, which we benchmark against its major PSU partner, as we

expect Dynapack to grow alongside its partner.
Risks
Our quantitative model assigns a High Risk rating to Dynapack. However, we believe such a rating is not warranted for
Dynapack due to: 1) the company is generating solid revenue and earnings growth over next 3 years; the underlying industry,
BBU for AIDC, also enjoys secular growth; 2) the business model that involves with product customization and strict
safety/reliability requirements would imply relatively benign competition and therefore stable pricing and margins; and 3) the

company has a strong balance sheet and has been in net cash position since 2019.
Key downside risks to our price target/rating are: 1) Slower-than-expected BBU adoption in AIDC, 2) Delay in PSU customer
qualifications, 3) Competition from larger battery or power solution vendors, 4) Safety, certification and reliability issues.
Panasonic Holdings
(6752.T; ¥3957.0; 1; 16 Oct 26; 15:30)
Valuation
To derive our target price, we use a global sum-of-the-parts (SoTP) approach. In the case of Panasonic Holdings, we reference

52 other companies in the Citi coverage universe to set a fair value PER for each business division, which we sum to derive our
overall target price.
Panasonic fair-value PERs for individual business segments range from 8x to 31x, and have a weighted average of 26.2x
(excluding subsidies in the battery business), taking minority interest and equity in earnings into account. We estimate
business-derived equity value of ¥5,000/share. We calculate the present value of the amount we expect Panasonic to receive

in battery subsidies over five years at ¥400/share. Combining these two values, we derive a target price of ¥5,400.
Risks
Risks to our target price include 1) unsuccessful management policies or strategies resulting in weaker-than-expected
business investment returns or sales growth, or conversely producing greater-than-expected results at a relatively early stage,
2) stagnation in the global economy or an upturn in it, and rises or declines in demand in the electrical industry, and 3) forex

trends. If these factors manifest themselves differently than we have anticipated, the share price may vary from our target
price.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be

our ratings distribution table for our Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside rating system. However, we reiterate that we do
not consider No View to be a recommendation. For all Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside calls, risk exists that the
catalyst(s) and associated share-price movement will not materialize as expected.

Gogolook三大事業體
資料來源：Gogolook、群益投顧彙整
⚫ Gogolook 是全球領先的信任科技 (TrustTech) 公司，運用自有防詐資料庫與 AI 技
術，提供消費者防詐、企業端防詐與金融科技服務。Gogolook 擁有全球最齊全的
通訊與數位防詐數據資料庫，涵蓋電話號碼、網址、和虛擬貨幣錢包地址等。

whoscall
⚫ whoscall 為 Gogolook 所
開發出的個人數位防詐
APP，全球下載量已突破
1億次，月活躍用戶超過
1,800萬，主要服務市場
涵蓋台灣、泰國、日本、

### Page 7

7
whoscall海外市場持續擴張
⚫ whoscall海外市場擴張成效卓越，海外營收占比於2025年已突破63%。泰國為
Gogolook最大的海外市場，其用戶規模、營收貢獻已超越台灣，但仍具備很大的
成長空間；公司於曼谷擁有龐大用戶基礎，目前正進軍泰國二、三線城市。菲律
賓與馬來西亞等地已具備百萬等級的活躍用戶，後續成長可期。
⚫ Gogolook於09/2026已正式和泰國政府退休基金(Government Pension Fund，GPF)簽
署MOU，並與國家跨行交易管理與交換公司(National ITMX)及泰國存款保障機構
(Deposit Protection Agency，DPA)合作，將whoscall防詐服務導入GPF體系，涵蓋
退休人員及現職公務員(超過120萬名會員)。此合作有助於同步推廣泰國當地B2C、
B2B業務。
⚫ 為了有效控制獲客成本，Gogolook在海外減少依賴成效較低的數位廣告，轉而與
當地政府、銀行及電信商合作。此外，考量到東南亞信用卡普及率較低，

Gogolook在泰國率先推出的「電信帳單扣款」訂閱模式大獲成功，目前此模式也
與台灣遠傳電信合作，藉此攻佔中高年齡層客群。

### Page 8

8
whoscall海外市場持續擴張
⚫ 針對品牌偏好較強或市場規模較小的國家(如日本與新加坡)，則透過白牌授權策略，
協助當地業者開發自有防詐App並從中分潤。
⚫ whoscall於02/2026~05/2026與日本Rakuten Mobile進行合作，推出「騷擾電話・簡
訊防護」活動，提供申請用戶免費試用，目前開始有試用用戶轉為付費訂閱，有
望挹注2H26日本市場消費者防詐業務成長動能。

### Page 10

10
Gogolook收購ScamAdviser
⚫ 企業端防詐方面，由於各國法規有加重企業防詐責任的趨勢，Gogolook將此業務
視為長期成長動能潛力來源。Gogolook於2024年收購具備超過6,000萬筆網域資料
庫的ScamAdviser後，成功拓展歐美市場。儘管2025年因主動調整部分低毛利率業
務，導致ScamAdviser營收下滑，但其實質獲利反而增加。
資料來源：Gogolook、群益投顧彙整
⚫ ScamAdviser成立於荷蘭阿姆斯
特丹，為全球防詐聯盟GASA
基石成員。ScamAdviser以AI演
算法與資料庫為基礎，開發自
有的網站風險評級技術，與
Gogolook的企業防詐解決方案
與電話號碼資料庫整合，為全
球最完整的通訊與數位防詐數
據資料庫。

### Page 11

11
Gogolook企業端防詐服務
⚫ 企業端服務主要分為「資料庫授權」與「商譽保護」兩大主軸：(1) 防詐情資方案，
為一種資料庫授權模式，將whoscall的「電話/簡訊」資料庫以及ScamAdviser的
「網域」資料庫，透過API、SDK方式授權給企業。(2) 商譽保護服務，企業的外
部防詐監控中心，協助企業或政府主動在網路上巡邏，偵測並下架冒用其品牌的
假投資廣告、假粉專或釣魚網站。
資料來源：Gogolook、群益投顧彙整

### Page 12

12
Agent looker
⚫ 因應Agentic AI時代來臨，Gogolook於09/2026推出新產品Agent looker，工作原理
是在AI agent實際造訪或讀取網頁前，即時進行風險檢測並攔截惡意請求，可有效
避免AI agent被詐騙的風險。
⚫ 核心防護機制與功能：(1)毫秒級即時掃描，當AI Agent讀取外部網頁或執行URL
請求時，Agent Looker會在毫秒內對網頁封包與內容進行深度檢查，攔截惡意腳本、
釣魚網站以及針對 AI 模型的提示詞注入(Prompt Injection)攻擊。(2)Gogolook威脅
情資資料庫驅動。系統底層串接 Gogolook 累積多年的全球防詐騙、釣魚網站與風
險網域黑名單。基於海量真實風險樣本的數據庫，使其能快速且精準地辨識異常
行為。(3)活動稽核日誌。提供視覺化的管理儀表板，完整記錄AI Agent的所有外
部網路互動軌跡，包含造訪過的網址、讀取的網頁片段及最終的安全判定結果，
確保AI運作透明且可追溯。
⚫ Agent looker目前處於封閉測試(Private Beta)階段。

資料來源：Gogolook、群益投顧彙整

### Page 13

13
金融科技-袋鼠金融
⚫ 金融科技為Gogolook於疫情期間因應線上詐騙激增而開創的新業務，包含全台灣
流量最大的金融媒合平台「袋鼠金融Roo.Cash」，以及專攻小額借款的「JUJI招財
麻吉」。袋鼠金融 Roo.Cash與國內金融機構合作，提供200件以上的金融商品方案
智慧比較與媒合。智能問答服務「Roo AI」，可協助用戶在信用卡、貸款、保險、
投資理財與民生優惠資訊中，快速找到最適合的選項。若順利成交，Gogolook即
可藉此獲得媒合收入。
資料來源：Gogolook、群益投顧彙整

AI風控模型，不僅可嚴格控制核貸率，更將90天延滯率壓在極低水準。目前JUJI
招財麻吉應收分期款餘額持續增加，公司同時也把純風控模型顧問服務輸出至海

Gogolook營業費用控管得宜

⚫ AI優化營運效率：利用AI提升工程與內部效率，人力控管但產出持續提升。

## Document 216: [005930] 삼성전자(005930) 현저한 저평가삼성전자(005930) 현저한 저평가현저한 저평가 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[005930] 삼성전자(005930) 현저한 저평가삼성전자(005930) 현저한 저평가현저한 저평가 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-06
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

00

삼성전자 (005930/KS) 
현저한 저평가 
3Q26 영업이익 106 조원 예상 
삼성전자의 3Q26 영업이익을 106조원 (+18% QoQ)으로 예상한다. 이는 기존 당사 
전망치 111 조원 대비 4.5% 하향된 것이다. 원/달러 환율 가정을 기존 1,450원에서 
1,400원으로 3.4% 하향, MX/NW 사업부의 적자 폭을 확대 반영 (-0.5조원 -> -
1.4조원)했기 때문이다. 주가의 핵심인 메모리 판가는 DRAM +17%, NAND +15%
로 오히려 예상을 소폭 상회할 것으로 예상된다. 출하는 수요 강세에도 불구하고 재고 
부족, HBM 확대에 따른 제한적 공급 여력으로 DRAM +4%, NAND +7%의 기존 예
상을 유지한다. HBM은 분기 최대 출하를 경신하기 시작할 것이다. 또한 AI 강세로 파
운드리 수주 및 가동률의 동반 상승이 지속 중이다. 성과 충당금 반영 등에 따라 단기 
흑자전환 가시성은 제한적이나, DS 사업부 전체적으로 강화되고 있는 국면이다.

2027 년 568 조원 (+55% YoY) 전망 유지. 환율 하락 반영 
SK증권은 삼성전자의 2027년 영업이익 전망치를 568조원으로 유지한다. 원/달러 환
율 가정을 기존 1,440원에서 1,340원으로 7% 하향했음에도, 실적 전망을 유지하는 
이유는 AI 메모리 수요 강세 속 공급 부족 심화, LTA에 따른 Dual market 효과에 의
한 Commodity 메모리 가격 강세, 2027년 HBM 판가 강세 (+103% YoY) 와 출하 
강세, 수익성 회복의 동반, 파운드리 적자 폭 축소의 본격화 전망 때문이다. 2027년 메
모리 출하는 DRAM +22%, NAND +16%, 판가는 DRAM +38%, NAND +28%
를 예상한다. 시장에서 우려하는 메모리 가격 하락, 감익 시나리오는 없을 것이다.

차별적 실적과 주주환원 본격화. 현저한 저평가 
SK증권은 메모리 업황과 삼성전자의 주가가 현저히 저평가 되어있다고 판단한다. 삼성
전자의 2027E P/E는 4.2X에 불과하며, 이익 창출력은 글로벌 최상위권이다. 주주환
원 정책 또한 기존의 FCF 50% 환원이 향후에도 유지될 것으로 가정한다면, 2027년 
193조원, 2028년 222조원으로 현재 시가총액 (우선주 포함) 대비 각각 11%, 13% 
수준에 육박한다. 성장을 위한 투자를 확대하고 있음에도, 대규모의 환원이 가능하다. 
LTA는 AI 시대 메모리 수요의 질과 실적 가시성을 제고시키며, 이는 주주환원의 지속
가능성을 의미한다. 메모리 가격의 높은 기저, LTA 물량의 계약 가격 적용을 감안하면, 
가격 상승률 자체는 안정화될 것이다. 다만 실적 안정성, 가시성, Duration을 확보하기 
시작했다는 점이 훨씬 중요한 가치이며, 아직 시장의 Playbook에 없을 뿐이다. 시대는 
변하고 있고, 시장도 변해야 한다. 투자의견 BUY, 목표주가 610,000원을 유지한다. 
2026-10-06

반도체. 한동희 / donghee.han@sks.co.kr / 3773-8826

## Document 276: Consumer Checkpoint_ Strength in numbers September saw solid spending across many categories and a strong start to the holidays. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Consumer Checkpoint_ Strength in numbers September saw solid spending across many categories and a strong start to the holidays. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13026023     1

Economy 
Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers 
09 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Consumer spending remained very solid in September. Bank of America aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), or 5.1% excluding gasoline, both the third strongest growth rates over the past four 
years. Month-over-month (MoM) spending was more muted, especially without gas. 
• Spending shows considerable breadth: the vast majority of discretionary retail and services categories are showing growth. 
Moreover, this strength extends across income cohorts, with the ongoing recovery of lower- and middle-income households' 
spending growth. 
• With the winter months fast approaching, holiday spending is already increasing at a solid rate, though it's still early. In Bank of 
America deposit data through September, buy now, pay later (BNPL) usage is rising but not at a rate that is overly concerning. 
However, nearly one in every five households that use BNPL has a repayment of over $500.

Consumer Checkpoint is a regular publication from Bank of America Institute. It aims to provide a holistic and real-time estimate of US 
consumers’ spending and their financial well-being, leveraging the depth and breadth of Bank of America proprietary data. Such data is not 
intended to be reflective or indicative of, and should not be relied upon as, the results of operations, financial conditions or performance of 
Bank of America. 
A solid September for spending   
Consumer spending continued to look solid in September. Bank of America total aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), while spending excluding gasoline increased 5.1% YoY. Notably, both are the third 
strongest growth rates in the past four years. Seasonally-adjusted total card spending rose 0.2% month-over-month (MoM), 
while spending excluding gasoline was little changed. Overall, spending growth remains well above the 2025 average rate 
(Exhibit 1). 
Exhibit 1: Total card spending per household rose 6.2% YoY and 5.1% YoY excluding gasoline in September 
Total card spending growth per household, based on Bank of America aggregated credit and debit card data (monthly, MoM%, seasonally adjusted (SA)) and 
(monthly, YoY%, non-SA, right-hand side (rhs))

Source: Bank of America internal data

Most spending categories are growing  
One striking thing about recent card spending, in our view, is that it is well balanced across categories. For example, both 
spending on retail (excluding gasoline) and services grew strongly YoY in September, at 3.5% and 5.4%, respectively. Meanwhile, 
retail spending is showing notably better momentum than in 2025 (Exhibit 2).  
Exhibit 2: Spending is rising across retail and services 
Retail (excluding gasoline) and services card spending per household (monthly, seasonally adjusted, index January 2025 = 100)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Well balanced across “nice-to-have” and “need-to-have” retail categories  
Is the momentum in retail spending reflective of “nice-to-have” discretionary or “need-to-have” necessity spending? In retail, 
this is a nuanced question, as many categories sit in a gray area between “necessity” and “discretionary”. General merchandise 
stores, for example, sell both essentials like groceries as well as nice-to-have electronics or even jewelry.  
In Exhibit 3, we account for this by grouping retail spending into “more discretionary” (furniture, electronics, building materials, 
online retailers, clothing retailers and miscellaneous stores), “partly discretionary” (auto, health and personal care retailers, 
general merchandise) and “mostly necessity” (gas and groceries). Even within these categories the edges will be blurred, but the 
overall impression is that retail spending growth is widely distributed, with spending rising across all fronts. One caveat: “mostly 
discretionary” spending has been making a smaller contribution since June. 
Exhibit 3: Retail spending is distributed across discretionary and necessity categories  
Percentage points contribution to YoY growth by type of retail spending (three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data. See methodology for category aggregation definitions.  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Looking at the share of “more discretionary” retail spending categories that are increasing YoY underscores that growth is well 
balanced and broad-based (Exhibit 4). Non-store and online retailers and miscellaneous stores saw some of the fastest growth, 
while furniture and building materials were among the laggards, though other durable categories showed some strength.

While some of this spending growth likely reflects the impact of higher prices pushing up spending across a swathe of 
categories, looking just at transactions as a proxy for volumes shows a similar picture, with only slightly less diffusion (Exhibit 5).  
Exhibit 4: Nearly 86% of categories are experiencing positive 
spending growth, accounting for over 94% of retail spending

(monthly, %)

Source: Bank of America internal data

growth, with durables responsible for the declines 
Diffusion index across “more discretionary” retail categories by

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

For services, growth is driven mostly by discretionary spending  
In services spending, on the other hand, the boundaries between discretionary and necessity spending are often clearer. Travel 
spending (e.g., airlines, lodging) can be thought of as, by and large, discretionary. At the same time, utility bills, rent and 
insurance payments are necessities.  
And in services, discretionary spending remains the clear driver, accounting for over three-quarters of YoY spending growth in 
September (Exhibit 6). Leisure and housing services are major components of growth, though travel and restaurants also made 
positive contributions. However, as we noted last month strength in airline spending largely reflects price rises rather than 
transactions growth (read more in Consumer Checkpoint: Still sizzling).  
Exhibit 6: Services spending growth has been mostly driven by discretionary spending over the past year 
Percentage points contribution to growth by type of services spending (three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data

Lower-income households’ spending outpaced all but the top 5% 
The breadth in spending growth across categories is also evident across income cohorts. In September, lower-income 
households’ total card spending growth was 5.5% YoY, while the equivalent figures for middle- and higher-income households 
were 4.9% and 4.8% YoY, respectively. The top 5% of households by income continue to show the fastest spending growth, 
5.9% in September, though this has eased somewhat (Exhibit 7). 
Overall, the divergences across income cohorts are much smaller than they were in 2025 and the first three months of this year. 
Just focusing on spending excluding key necessities (gasoline, food and utilities) doesn’t meaningfully change the picture of 
convergence across income cohorts (Exhibit 8).  
Exhibit 7: The top 5% saw the strongest spending growth of any 
income cohort… 
Total card spending per household by income tercile and the top 5% 
(three-month moving average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 8: …though discretionary spending for the top 5% has eased 
some since June 
Discretionary card spending per household by income tercile and the top 
5% (three-month moving average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The strength in lower- and middle-income households’ discretionary spending is occurring despite necessity spending shifting 
from a tailwind to a headwind over the past year amid rising gas prices (Exhibit 9). In our view, a key component of why these 
households have been able to combine rising necessity spending with strength in discretionary is the labor market and a 
recovery in their after-tax wage growth (Exhibit 10).  
In September, lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY from 4.7% YoY in August, while growth for middle- and higher-
income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. These changes were modest, and lower-income households are 
continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” pattern we 
observed over much of 2025 and 2026.  
Exhibit 9: Necessity spending has gone from a tailwind to a 
headwind across all income cohorts 
Necessity card spending per household by income tercile and the top 5% 
(three-month moving average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 10: Lower-income wage growth remains strongest, despite 
a narrowing gap 
After-tax wage and salary growth by household income tercile (three-
month moving average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

### Page 5

I NSTI TUTE

Should we be concerned about the easing in household spending and after-tax wage growth for those with the top 5% of 
incomes? For now, probably not, as in our view this spending growth is likely being driven by wealth effects rather than wage 
gains. Absent a sharp correction in equity markets, there is little reason to expect them to retrench their spending. However, 
developments here are worth watching as this cohort comprises a significant proportion of overall consumer spending. They also 
tend to make outsized contributions to several discretionary spending categories (Exhibit 11).  
Exhibit 11: The top 5% of households by income make up a disproportionate amount of spending in highly discretionary categories 
Share of spending by the top 5% of households by income across select discretionary categories (three-month moving average to September 2026, %)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
With summer gone, thoughts turn to the holidays and BNPL… 
Now that summer is over, the next big gauge of consumer momentum is how people spend during the run-up to, and over, the 
holiday season. It is early days, but September spending on holiday items ran well ahead of 2025 (Exhibit 12). Given the backdrop 
of firmer after-tax wage growth and breadth across retail and services categories, this is perhaps not surprising. However, some 
of this spending strength could be due to rising prices as opposed to more purchases.  
Exhibit 12: It’s early days, but holiday spending is strong so far this year 
Card spending per household for holiday items by year (seven-day moving average, index September 2025 average = 1)

A concern often raised as we move into the holidays is whether consumers will lean on less traditional forms of credit such as 
buy now, pay later (BNPL) to finance their spending. Frequent BNPL users tend to have higher credit card utilization rates (read 
more in: Do consumers have wiggle room to absorb higher gas prices?).  
Looking at the share of households in Bank of America deposit data with a BNPL payment, we do tend to see a seasonal rise 
over the holidays (Exhibit 13), though in past years, these increases have quickly reversed. Further, while the underlying trend in 
BNPL usage is upwards, there are no signs of a sharp acceleration. We do, however, see some evidence that BNPL is being used 
to finance more expensive purchases, with the share of households making repayments above $500 rising, albeit relatively slowly 
(Exhibit 14).  
Exhibit 13: 8.3% of Bank of America households had a BNPL

The tendency towards greater usage is occurring across income cohorts, though is somewhat more pronounced for lower-
income households (Exhibit 15). However, higher-income households have the largest share of BNPL payments above $500 
(Exhibit 16). Overall, in our view, the increase in the share of BNPL users heading into the season does not appear too 
concerning, though the rate of usage for some BNPL consumers does bear watching.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Store category aggregation for “more discretionary”, “partly discretionary”, and “mostly necessity” is defined as follows: 
More discretionary: furniture, electronics, building materials, hobbies, miscellaneous stores, nonstore retail, clothing. 
Partly discretionary: auto, health and personal care, general merchandise. 
Mostly necessity: Gasoline, food and beverage.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts. 
The “more discretionary” retail category includes furniture, electronics, building materials, online marketplaces, clothing retailers and 
miscellaneous stores; while “partly discretionary” retail contains auto parts and services, health and personal care retailers, general merchandise 
and “mostly necessity”  retail contains gas and groceries. 
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

## Document 301: First Impressions_ RBNZ Monetary Policy Review October 2026 (2026-10-01)

### Page 1

#rbnz #newzealand
First Impressions: RBNZ Monetary Policy Review October 2026
By Kelly Eckhold & Westpac New Zealand Economics
12:30 October 28 2026 Share
The RBNZ left the OCR unchanged at 2.75% (in contrast to market expectation for a hike,
including ourselves).
The MPC has stuck to its strategy of pausing to assess the impact of tightening already done. There is
a clear split among the MPC members on where the risks lie although it seems clear enough that

higher interest rates are in the future the data evolves as the Committee expects. Key factors to focus
on are data on inflation expectations, the global economy (and potential downside risks there) and the

### Page 2

path of fuel prices.  We expect a 25 bp increase in the OCR in December to 3% and are reverting to
the OCR profile we had up until mid last week.  We don't think the December hike is a done deal as the
data will inform the next move. We will revert with more detail after the press conference. 
Key takeouts from the minutes below.

Media contact
Kelly Eckhold
Chief economist 
+64 9 348 9382
+64 21 786 758
Key takeouts
Category 2 September 2026 meeting 28 October 2026 meeting
The next policy
decision
Future policy decisions will depend
on the Committee’s judgement of the
balance of risks to medium-term
inflation.
Members discussed that information
arriving over the next six weeks
could meaningfully change the
appropriate policy stance in either
direction.
  Conditional on the central economic
outlook, members judged that the
OCR may need to increase further.
All members agreed that tighter
monetary policy is likely to be
required, but remains conditional on
the economic outlook.
  However, the future OCR path is not
pre-determined.

The medium term
direction for the
OCR
The Committee judges that gradually
removing monetary stimulus is
appropriate to return inflation to the
2 percent target mid-point while
supporting growth and employment.
Further OCR increases likely, but
are conditional on the outlook

### Page 3

Category 2 September 2026 meeting 28 October 2026 meeting
  All members agreed that gradually
removing monetary stimulus is
consistent with achieving the
medium-term inflation target,
conditional on the outlook.
If domestic pricing pressures fail to
moderate sufficiently or broaden
beyond what costs and normal pass-
through can explain, this could
warrant further tightening.
  Conditional on the central economic
outlook, members judged that the
OCR may need to increase further.
On the other hand, subdued pricing
pressures could require holding at
future meetings.
    And if the global economy turns
sharply down, materially weakening
demand and the inflation outlook,
this could alter the desired stance,
including leading to monetary
easing.

The neutral OCR   The Committee acknowledged that
the neutral interest rate has a wide
range of plausible estimates.
    Members noted that the increase in
global long-term yields may partly
signal a higher global neutral
interest rate, due to higher inflation
expectations, war destruction and
demand for capital to support AI
investment.
    Higher global interest rates could
bring forward a potential global
downturn, and the neutral interest
rate could decline rapidly in the
event of an abrupt global
adjustment.

### Page 4

Category 2 September 2026 meeting 28 October 2026 meeting

The strength of
economic activity
After lacklustre growth in the June
quarter, New Zealand's economic
recovery has most likely resumed
but remains uneven.
The New Zealand economy
continues to recover, although
conditions remain uneven.
  The recovery is expected to
strengthen and broaden.
The Committee noted that GDP
growth was more than expected
through the first half of 2026.
  The Committee assessed that
growth was lacklustre in the second
quarter of 2026.
Business survey measures point to
continued expansion in economic
activity.
  The economic recovery is expected
to have resumed in the third quarter,
while remaining uneven across
sectors and regions.
Household consumption has been
weak and the aggregate saving rate
is estimated to be 3.7 percent,
above the 10-year average of 1
percent.

The global
economic
outlook
The global economy is facing
significant risks that could affect
commodity prices and demand for
exports.
Global growth has been resilient but
is increasingly fragile given
geopolitical tensions, high energy
prices, financial stresses, and
surging AI-related investment.
  Despite recent geopolitical events,
economic growth in New Zealand’s
trading partners has remained
resilient and the outlook has
improved.
The Committee assessed that there
are significant risks to the global
economic outlook.

### Page 5

Category 2 September 2026 meeting 28 October 2026 meeting
  Although risks to the global economy
remain heightened, the Committee
expects global economic conditions
to remain favourable for New
Zealand’s export facing businesses
over the medium term.
The Committee noted that a global
downturn could occur abruptly.

The labour
market
Conditions in the labour market
should improve as the recovery
gathers pace.
Employment growth remains
subdued and higher prices will
further reduce real incomes and
purchasing power.
  Employment growth has not been
sufficient to fully absorb new
entrants into the labour market and
unemployment is elevated,
particularly in Auckland and
Wellington and for youth and the
long-term unemployed.
Employment intentions remain
weak, pointing to continued slow
recovery in the labour market.
  The labour market is expected to
slowly improve, with employment
growing and unemployment falling
as the economy recovers.
To date, wage growth has been
subdued, and medium-term inflation
expectations have remained stable.
  On balance, the Committee
assesses that spare capacity
remains in the economy, particularly
in the labour market.

### Page 6

Category 2 September 2026 meeting 28 October 2026 meeting
The short term
inflation outlook
Inflation increased to 4.1 percent in
the June quarter because of higher
fuel prices arising from the conflict in
the Middle East.
Annual inflation is expected to
increase to 4.4 percent in the
December quarter, largely reflecting
current futures prices for fuel.
  The Committee expects inflation to
remain elevated this year before
returning to the target band by mid-
2027 and reaching the 2 percent
mid-point later next year.
Near-term inflation forecasts are
uncertain given volatility in prices for
oil and refined fuels.
    Direct effects of higher fuel prices
will drop out of the annual inflation
calculation in late 2027 if prices for
refined petroleum products stabilise
or fall.

The medium term
inflation outlook
Core inflation, expected wage
growth, and inflation expectations
remain consistent with inflation
returning to the 1 to 3 percent target
band by mid-2027 and the 2 percent
target midpoint later next year.
The Committee is focused on
returning inflation to the 2 percent
target mid-point over the medium
term, discounting temporary
disturbances to inflation and
avoiding unnecessary economic
instability.
  Longer-term inflation expectations
remain near 2 percent, while most
measures of one and two-year-
ahead inflation expectations have
fallen since May.
Impacts on shortterm pricing
behaviour appear to have been
contained and medium-term inflation
expectations remain well anchored.
  Currently, indicators of medium-term
inflation are consistent with inflation
returning to target.
But the longer inflation remains high,
the greater the risk of higher costs
feeding into inflation expectations
and price- and wage-setting
behaviour, making inflation more
persistent.

### Page 7

Category 2 September 2026 meeting 28 October 2026 meeting

The balance of
risks for
economic growth
and inflation
New Zealand’s economic recovery
could be stronger or weaker than
expected and price pressures could
generate more persistent inflation.
New Zealand’s recovery could be
stronger than expected but could
also lose momentum if the global
environment deteriorates or export
strength does not flow through to
jobs, incomes and household
spending.
  Consumption growth could be
subdued if employment and house
price growth remain weak, or if
precautionary behaviour persists.
There is a risk that inflation becomes
entrenched before conventional
expectations measures respond,
despite the previous two OCR
increases.
  Alternatively, consumption growth
could be stronger and absorb spare
capacity faster than currently
anticipated.
In addition, the world economy could
tip abruptly into a downturn causing
weaker inflation over the medium
term.
  All members agreed that downside
risks to activity were significant and
that the recovery could remain
uneven.
It is also possible that geopolitical
escalation could drive fuel prices
higher while weakening global
economic activity at the same time.

Key arguments
advanced by
more hawkish
MPC members
Hayley Gourley, Karen Silk,
Prasanna Gai and Anna Breman
saw upside risks to inflation relative
to the central projection.
Hayley Gourley, Paul Conway and
Karen Silk favoured increasing the
OCR by 25 basis points at this
meeting.

### Page 8

Category 2 September 2026 meeting 28 October 2026 meeting
  These members observed that more
persistence in energy and
petrochemical prices could raise
near-term inflation risks, impact
price-setting behaviour, and lead to
more sticky inflation over the
medium term.
These members emphasised that
core inflation has been above levels
consistent with headline inflation at
the target mid-point for some time.
  Price-setting can be a front-loaded
process, meaning businesses could
raise domestic prices more than
import costs warrant.
This increases the risk of inflation
becoming entrenched in price-
setting behaviour as the conflict and
its consequences endure, including
through higher freight and shipping
costs.
  These factors increase the risk that
monetary policy needs to lean
against broader inflation pressures.
Acting now would guard against
sustained inflation pressures and
potentially reduce the risk of needing
to do more with interest rates later.

Key arguments
advanced by
more dovish MPC
members in each
meeting
Paul Conway and Carl Hansen saw
risks to inflation as balanced.
Anna Breman, Carl Hansen,
Rebecca Williams and Prasanna Gai
favoured holding the OCR at this
meeting as two recent OCR
increases allow the Committee to
take further time to assess their
effects and economic developments.
  These members shared concerns
that high inflation could become
embedded through price-setting
behaviour.
Actual inflation was lower than
expected, reflecting smaller indirect
effects of higher fuels prices, and
medium-term inflation expectations
and wage growth remain consistent
with returning inflation to the target
mid-point.

Category 2 September 2026 meeting 28 October 2026 meeting
But weighed against this, they saw
activity risks as skewed to the
downside and noted that these could
weigh on inflation.
These members emphasised the
risk of a weak recovery, particularly
in household consumption and
investment.
They highlighted the potential effects
of weak house prices and
precautionary behaviour, weakening
growth in household spending.
They favoured pausing at this

meeting to observe and assess
demand and financial conditions.

### Page 4

◆ The economy remains on the recovery track despite weakness in some
areas
Annualized real GDP growth in the second quarter was +1.1% QoQ, with growth
staying in positive territory for three straight quarters. Although personal
consumption exhibited weakness and capital investment declined, overall growth
was lifted by foreign demand. Exports, particularly of services, increased, and the
drop in imports of Middle Eastern crude oil also helped foreign demand turn positive.

◆ Housing starts are weak
Housing starts surged 18.6% YoY in June. They have continued to rise on a YoY
basis, but the jump was mainly due to a rebound from the slump in the same month
of the previous year. The recovery in the number of starts is actually moderate,
reflecting higher interest rates and soaring construction costs.
◆ Earthquake impact will need to be watched
Damage from the earthquake that occurred in Kumamoto Prefecture at the end of

July is weighing on economic activity in the affected areas. Although moves toward
recovery can be seen, with many companies having already resumed operations,
the impact could become prolonged in the following scenarios:
The first is if damage to capital stock such as factories and roads has been severe.
Some companies have sustained damage to production facilities, making an early
resumption of operations difficult in some cases.
The second is if a downturn in tourism demand occurs. According to some media

reports, there has been an uptick in cancellations of accommodation reservations in
Kumamoto Prefecture and neighboring prefectures since the earthquake. After the
2016 Kumamoto earthquake, the decline in travel demand from Japanese visitors
was short lived, but lower demand from foreign tourists persisted for a long period.
Annualized real GDP growth was +1.1% quarter over quarter (QoQ) in Q2 2026

### Page 5

Monthly Report of Prospects for Japan's Economy August 2026
The Japan Research Institute, Limited
Exports and inbound demand remain solid despite weakness in some areas
◆ Goods exports are on the verge of picking up
Goods exports are treading water. A sharp rise in memory chip prices is among
factors pushing up export prices, resulting in record high levels of exports in value
terms recently. On the other hand, the increase in volume terms is limited. One

possible reason is that Japan's export competitiveness has declined in fields such as
semiconductors, where global demand is growing.
Looking ahead, exports are expected to pick up. Capital investment by chipmakers
is expanding worldwide, and this should lift exports of semiconductor manufacturing
equipment, an area in which Japan has strength. However, continued caution is
needed regarding the effects of prolonged turmoil in the Middle East. A global

slowdown due to elevated crude oil prices could weigh on exports.
◆ Reluctance to visit among Chinese travelers is weighing on demand for
travel to Japan
Inbound demand is currently soft. The Chinese government's advisory urging its
citizens to refrain from traveling to Japan is having an adverse impact. The number
of Chinese visitors to Japan has fallen to about half the level prior to the advisory.
Meanwhile, the number of tourists from countries other than China remains firm.

Inbound demand is expected to stay robust going forward. Continued increases in
tourists from countries other than China will remain a supporting factor. The
expectation that the yen will remain weak will also act to push up inbound
consumption. However, there are two downside risks going forward:
The first is that the Chinese government keeps the advisory in place for a
prolonged period. When China banned group travel to South Korea in 2017, tourist

numbers stayed low for two to three years.
The second is the impact of higher crude oil prices. Major airlines have embarked
on phased increases in fuel surcharges, which could weigh on inbound demand
during the summer.

### Page 6

Personal consumption is gradually recovering thanks to a favorable income environment
◆ Higher disposable incomes are supporting personal consumption
Personal consumption continues to gradually recover. While consumption of
non-durable goods remains weak, services consumption is firm, particularly in
amusement-related services. In addition, consumption of durable goods has
increased significantly. Temporary factors such as the abolition of the environmental

performance tax on automobiles (since April 2026) and the tightening of
energy-efficiency standards for air conditioners (effective April 2027) have also
contributed to this rise.
A favorable income environment can also be pointed to as a background factor
behind the recovery in personal consumption. YoY real disposable income growth has
generally been positive since mid-2024, and the pace of increase has recently
accelerated. Employee remuneration is rising, partly as a result of agreements made

during the shunto (spring wage negotiations), and net asset income is also increasing
at a faster clip as companies hike dividends in line with stock price rises.
◆ Stock price developments could be a source of concern for personal
consumption
Personal consumption is expected to continue its gradual recovery going forward. In
addition to the favorable income environment and government policy measures to
curb price increases, a recovery in consumer confidence driven by easing tensions in

the Middle East will also provide a tailwind. Recently, the Consumer Confidence Index
has been showing signs of bottoming out.
However, a reverse wealth effect stemming from falling stock prices is a potential
cause for concern. The Nikkei Stock Average, which reached a new high in June, has
been struggling to push higher since the start of July. Estimates suggest the wealth
effect is concentrated among elderly households, but in recent years working-age

households have also increased their holdings of equity-related assets, and the
positive wealth effect for them may have supported consumption to some extent. For
this reason, if stock prices were to fall significantly, there is a danger of downward
pressure on consumption across a wide range of households.

### Page 7

セン

◆ The unemployment rate has settled in the mid-2% range
While the employment situation is generally favorable, weakness can be seen in
some areas. The unemployment rate has remained in the mid-2% range since 2022.
The number of employed persons in non-manufacturing, where business conditions
are favorable, is holding firm, while in manufacturing the number remains at a level
below that seen before the COVID-19 pandemic.
In the background to the drop in manufacturing employment is a slump in

production activity. Manufacturing output has been shrinking due to the effects of
U.S. tariff policy, soaring raw material prices, and a decline in the international
competitiveness of Japanese firms. As a result, pressure to reduce headcounts has
been building. Even in non-manufacturing, some industries closely linked to
manufacturing, such as transportation and wholesale/retail, are also exhibiting
weakness in labor demand.
◆ The employment situation could remain fragile in the near term

Looking ahead at the employment situation in the short term, a sense of
stagnation may persist. The contraction in output in the manufacturing sector will
continue to weigh on labor demand. Although production activity has been brisker
recently, particularly in semiconductor-related industries, the employment-creation
capacity of such industries is small. The employment-inducement coefficient per unit
of new demand tends to be lower than in other sectors, meaning that the industries

lack the strength to serve as drivers of employment.
However, in the medium to long term, the unemployment rate is expected to see a
gradual decline. Demand for labor is projected to stay solid, especially in
face-to-face services such as medical/nursing care and accommodation and
eating/drinking services, but the number of people seeking employment from the
non-labor force population has declined significantly due to the expansion in elderly
and female workforce participation. With limited room for expansion in the labor

supply, the job market is expected to tighten further.

The government is curbing inflation
◆ Policy factors are pushing down the inflation rate
In June, core inflation (all items, less fresh food) was 1.6%, staying under 2%
for the fifth consecutive month. Food price inflation has slowed, and a major

reason for this is a fallback in rice prices, which soared last year. Gasoline
subsidies are also continuing to curtail energy price increases.
Inflation looks set to hover in the 2% range in the second half of 2026. Although
energy subsidies and other government measures will soften inflationary pressure
to some extent, with imported inflationary pressure building, prices are expected
to be pushed up as higher costs are passed through to selling prices. From

FY2027 onward, the reduction in the consumption tax rate for food items will
serve to keep prices down, and inflation is expected to drop into the 1% range.
◆ The Bank of Japan (BOJ) will keep its policy rate unchanged
At its July monetary policy meeting, the BOJ decided to keep its policy rate
unchanged.
Long-term interest rates remain high. This reflects concerns about inflation
resulting from the increase in oil prices and expectations of an expansion in fiscal

expenditure.
The BOJ is expected to step up the pace of interest rate hikes while closely
monitoring the impact of higher rates on the real economy and financial/capital
markets. The next rate hike is expected to be in September.
Long-term interest rates look likely to rise gradually, reflecting heightened
anticipation of rate hikes by the BOJ and increased fiscal spending by the
government. If jitters about a deterioration in fiscal discipline resulting from such

measures as energy subsidies or the consumption tax cut take hold, there is also
a risk that long-term interest rates will rise.

◆ Surging crude oil prices have become a source of upward pressure on
prices
With crude oil prices having spiked against the backdrop of the worsening situation
in the Middle East, corporate input costs have risen sharply, and pressure for price
pass-through is intensifying. Looking at the Final Demand-Intermediate Demand
price indexes, the margin of price increases at the upstream stage far exceeds that at
the downstream stage, and price pass-through pressure is also substantial at the

downstream stage.
According to estimates based on past data, the impact of rising oil prices involves
a time lag. Specifically, the push-up effect on the consumer price inflation rate
increases for around 10 months and continues to exert influence for a long period
thereafter.
◆ Price pass-through willingness is also stronger than before
Notably, food product manufacturers are planning to raise prices for a huge
number of items during the summer and beyond. According to Teikoku Databank, the

number of items slated for price increases as of the end of July, when it conducted its
latest survey, was higher than in the May and June surveys.
If government subsidies for electricity/gas bills and gasoline prices are scaled back
or ended, both energy and food prices will increase concurrently, and the consumer
price inflation rate may rise again.
Attention also needs to be paid to the possibility that price pass-through driven by
elevated oil prices will become prolonged. In the 2020s, the rate of price increases in

transactions between companies has been higher than in the 2010s, and firms'
medium-term outlooks for selling prices have also shifted upward. Events that trigger
cost increases, such as heightened geopolitical tensions, have been occurring
repeatedly, and companies may be more willing to pass through higher costs to
prices than they were before.

### Page 10

The economy is expected to continue to recover, but downside risks are present
◆ The economy is projected to stay on the recovery track
The Japanese economy should continue its gradual recovery. WTI crude oil
futures prices are expected to fall moderately going forward.
In the corporate sector, government support for investment is providing a tailwind,
and demand for investment for labor saving and digital transformation remains
robust. In addition, rising global demand for AI-related goods should support exports.

Supply chain disruptions resulting from the deterioration in the Middle East situation
will begin to ease, and corporate production activity is expected to gradually recover.
In the household sector, inflationary pressure is expected to strengthen from the
summer onward due to higher crude oil prices. In response, the government will
probably ease the inflationary pressure through measures such as energy subsidies.
While the pace of increase in personal consumption is likely to slow somewhat,

consumption is expected to remain resilient amid continued high wage growth. From
FY2027 onward, the reduction in the consumption tax rate for food items should also
support personal consumption.
◆ The Middle East situation and rising interest rates constitute risks
Real GDP growth is projected to remain solid, registering +0.8% in FY2026 and
+0.9% in FY2027.
However, if the situation in the Middle East worsens again or becomes prolonged,
there is a risk that downward pressure on the economy will intensify, mainly through

three channels: 1) weaker exports, 2) raw material supply constraints, and 3) rising
prices. In addition, if market concerns about the fiscal stance of the Takaichi
government grow, the yen could weaken further, and long-term interest rates could
rise, potentially forcing the scaling-back or scrapping of expansionary policies such
as investment support measures, energy subsidies, and lower consumption tax.

## Document 346: Next Generation EU_ The programme is coming to an end, but its effects on economic activity will endure (2026-09-23)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Next Generation EU_ The programme is coming to an end, but its effects on economic activity will endure (2026-09-23).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

23 September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
NEXT GENERATION EU: THE PROGRAMME IS COMING TO AN END, BUT ITS EFFECTS ON ECONOMIC ACTIVITY WILL
ENDURE
Guillaume Derrien
A pillar of the NextGenerationEU programme, the Recovery and Resilience Facility (RRF) will expire on December 31, 2026. As of the
end of August 2026, slightly more than three-quarters of the total budget had been disbursed (EUR 440 billion out of EUR 573 billion).
Disbursing the remaining quarter (EUR 133 billion) by the end of the year will require a significant increase compared with the histo-

rical pace (EUR 90 billion per year). The goal of channeling these funds toward productive investment appears to have been achieved.
Tangible results for investment in Europe
According to Eurostat, public spending co-financed by the RRF totaled EUR 310 billion between 2020 and 2025. Nearly three-quarters (74%)
consisted of capital expenditures, including EUR 106 billion in direct public investment and EUR 122 billion in capital transfers to the private

sector (such as grants). This spending supported investment by businesses and households.
The list of major programmes funded by the RFF, published by the European Commission 1, shows that, within this scope, nearly two-thirds of
the funding went to transportation (primarily rail), energy infrastructure, and digital infrastructure (see Chart 1).
Another notable point: capital expenditures co-financed by the RRF increased significantly, reaching 0.4% of GDP for the European Union as a

whole in 2025 (see Chart 2). There are significant disparities among Member States, primarily due to differences in the amounts allocated to
them. Croatia (1.9%), Greece (1.2%), Italy (1%), and Spain (0.7%) received significant support, in contrast to France and Germany (0.1%). These
figures are consistent with the IMF’s estimate that the RRF added approximately 0.3 percentage points (pp) to EU growth in 2025 (more than
1 pp in Greece and Croatia, around 0.8 pp in Italy, and 0.5 pp in Spain) 2. Furthermore, as the first major joint European borrowing initiative, the

RRF made it possible to finance investment without increasing national budget deficits, giving several countries (Spain, Italy, Greece, Portugal)
valuable breathing room to consolidate their public finances.
1 Recovery and Resilience Scoreboard
2 The EU’s Recovery and Resilience Facility: Experience and Lessons for the Next Multiannual Financial Framework, WP/26/114, June 2026

Chart of the Week / 23 September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
What impact on EU’s economic activity can we expect once this programme ends?
At first glance, effects will be limited. The end of the programme is expected to weigh on both public and private investment. However, the
strengthening of economic activity that we anticipate (see details in our latest Ecoperspectives – Q3 2026) rests primarily on German public

investment programmes and structural drivers (investment in AI, defence spending).
The ECB makes the same point in its September macroeconomic projections 3. It emphasises that the end of the RRF is expected to weigh
on public investment in 2027, while it anticipates an acceleration in business investment. Furthermore, RRF funds are being absorbed on
the ground only gradually. Since most disbursements are concentrated toward the end of the programme, the effects of the RRF will extend

beyond 2026.

## Document 351: October ECB cheat sheet_ French malaise shouldn’t prompt a dovish turn

Source PDF: all report pdf/October ECB cheat sheet_ French malaise shouldn’t prompt a dovish turn.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-27
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Markets will scan the European Central Bank announcement on
29 October for any signs that French bond turmoil has
triggered a dovish rethink. We don’t think it has, and expect
the ECB to leave the door open for a December hike, which
should shield rates and the euro from a major adjustment. As
usual, we also look at three alternative scenarios to our
baseline

Francesco Pesole, Michiel Tukker and Carsten Brzeski
The ECB is expected to leave rates on hold this week at 2.5%
This is our market preview of October's European Central
Bank meeting; you can ﬁnd our macro preview here.
In September, the ECB eﬀectively brushed aside rising bond market
concerns with a hawkish rate hike. This week, a hold is widely
expected. The key question is whether the latest turmoil in French

and other European bonds is enough to trigger a dovish shift in the
ECB’s message.
We don’t think we are there yet. But equally, we admit that a more
dovish tone on further tightening would probably be the ﬁrst line of
defence against more bond volatility, before exploring any bond-
buying measure.
Four scenarios for October
As usual, we look at four scenarios for this meeting. As in previous
instances, the market reaction will depend as much on the

statement and press conference as on the now-customary tip to
the media on Thursday afternoon. In September, the message was:
“ECB oﬃcials expect more tightening, with October in play”.
A hike this week would, however, be a big surprise (close to zero
chance priced in), as inﬂation has remained well-behaved, France
has prompted a dovish repricing and President Christine Lagarde
has sounded a bit less hawkish.
In a meeting without new projections, the wording around

December will be crucial. We expect the door to remain open for a
December move, with a broadly neutral and balanced message to
markets. Signalling that a hike by year-end is “likely” would be a
slightly hawkish surprise in our view, even if markets are pricing in
20bp.

### Page 3

Rates look for predictability amid fragile
sentiment
Rates have started to deviate from their tight correlation with oil,
but global factors remain an important driver. Risk sentiment is
more fragile, and not just because of the French turmoil, but also
because of renewed AI jitters. So whilst markets will listen carefully
to Lagarde, we don’t expect this week’s meeting to trigger a major
repricing. A stronger dedication to a December hike would nudge a

hawkish repricing, but with more than 20bp already priced in, the
reaction should be contained. Both the ECB and markets
understand the outlook is shrouded in uncertainty, so any forward
guidance will be weighed against that backdrop.
A bigger surprise to markets would be a 25bp hike, but this would
not be taken lightly by investors, especially if December remains in
play too. Without clear second-round inﬂation risks, an unexpected
tightening of ﬁnancial conditions while sentiment is fragile could

trigger a broader risk-oﬀ move. Expect French government bond
spreads to widen and equities to continue their drift lower. We
might even see 10yr Bund yields fall on the decision, as growth
concerns return.
EUR could do with some ECB protection
The euro has become less sensitive to the latest moves in French
bond spreads, with undervaluation to short-term fair value still
around 1-1.5%. Still, the common currency has been hurt not only
by the build-up of a ﬁscal risk premium, but also by the repricing

lower in ECB rate expectations because of the French turmoil.
This week’s meeting is unlikely to have the same FX impact as
September’s, but currency investors will be looking for any cracks in
the ECB’s tightening bias as a sign that bond market concerns
could prompt a dovish rethink.
That probably leaves the balance of risks on the downside for the
euro. Still, our baseline is for no pushback against lingering hawkish
bets, leaving the euro broadly stable after Thursday.

## Document 358: Postcard from Bangkok IMF meetings_ don’t take resilience for granted (2026-10-19)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Postcard from Bangkok IMF meetings_ don’t take resilience for granted (2026-10-19).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-19
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026EDITORIAL
POSTCARD FROM BANGKOK IMF MEETINGS: DON’T TAKE RESILIENCE FOR GRANTED
1 See EcoNews for more details.
The global economy’s unexpected resilience might have invited the self-congratulation that is a trademark of internatio-
nal official meetings. But policymakers gathered in Bangkok for the 2026 IMF Annual Meetings were wearily aware that
“continued resilience cannot be taken for granted”, as noted in the prologue to the closing Chair’s Statement to the Inter-

national Monetary and Financial Committee meeting. Growth forecasts remain in line with those made a year ago, when
interest rates were much lower and no energy shock was in the cards, and even with those made in April, when a short
shock was the base case. But what has sustained global growth against the odds so far could falter before the headwinds
ease: oil buffers are being depleted; fiscal policy urgently needs to shift from support to deficit reduction as interest rates

remain persistently higher; and the AI boom that has been a salvatory growth engine comes with serious questions about
its sustainability and long-term economic effects. In addition to these global issues, the combination of fiscal challenges
and elections in France and the US were seen as key risks, while some topics that had until recently been in the spotlight
found themselves overshadowed.
BETTER GROWTH THAN EXPECTED FOR MOST , WORSE INFLATION FOR ALL

Despite the energy shock and surge in interest rates, the IMF's
latest global growth forecast is unchanged from a year ago and April
— at 3.1% in 2026 and 3.4% in 2027 — although the geographical
breakdown has shifted: war-exposed countries and many energy im -
porters have been downgraded, while AI-exposed countries have been
upgraded. Asia is the standout winner, expected to account for 68% of
global growth in 2026 — a feat for a region highly dependent on Gulf

energy. Compared to July, the growth forecast for advanced economies
is revised up to 2.1% in 2026 (+0.4pp, driven by Germany) and remains
at 1.8% in 2027. Among emerging markets, the IMF upgraded Asia and
downgraded other regions. Meanwhile, global headline inflation is ex-
pected to rise to 4.7% in 2026, mainly due to energy and food, before
easing to 4.1% in 2027 (revised +0.2pp)1.
AI OPTIMISM DOMINATES, BUT WITH SOME HEAVY UNANSWERED
QUESTIONS

The topic of AI dominated the Meetings. The broad consensus was that
it is transformational and offers great opportunities to all countries.
But, for now, it is producing a mixed bag of economic effects: a power-
ful boost to global demand, directly through an investment and trade
boom and indirectly through wealth effects from stock-market gains;
but also much higher interest rates (fuelled by the borrowing needed
to fund the AI build-out, and higher prices for chips, all the products

they go into, and the electricity and water data centres need).
Beyond this shared diagnosis, strong arguments could be heard on both
sides of several critical questions. The most pressing being: will AI ge-
nerate earnings commensurate with current expectations? If not — or
even if expectations are merely downgraded — expect large declines in
AI-related asset prices, a sudden stop in funding and a significant blow
to growth wherever AI has been a key engine. This is not in anyone’s

baseline for next year, but it is clearly a top risk. To what extent will AI
boost productivity over five or ten years? The IMF incorporates almost
no boost in its five-year baseline, but presents an upside scenario in
which it does. Will AI create more jobs than it destroys, raise or reduce
labour’s share of income, prove inflationary or deflationary beyond the
near term, and strengthen cyber resilience or turbocharge attacks?
And how should it be taxed to preserve the fiscal base and offset unde-

sirable labour-market effects?
A MIXED POLICY SCORECARD
Monetary policy has appropriately taken a hawkish turn nearly eve -
rywhere, and an increase in rate rises is likely in key economies, though
not enough to threaten growth. Eurozone central bankers were parti -
cularly clear in sticking to their “moderate response” playbook.
Fiscal policy, however, has yet to adjust to the end of cheap debt. With
public debt high in most countries, a growing share of national bud -

gets will be absorbed by interest payments. Yet very few countries are
consolidating. The IMF’s top call to action was to address this urgent-
ly, especially among the worst offenders — the US and France. Fiscal
restraint would also help central banks curb inflation, reducing the
need for higher interest rates.
Policies to redress global imbalances are not closer to being adop -
ted than at previous IMF Meetings, but they were not particular -
ly highlighted by the IMF this time, beyond the mantra that “the US

needs to save more, China to consume more, and Europe to invest
more”. There were reportedly good discussions behind closed doors
— at the G7 on “non-market policies and practices (NMPPs)” — and
encouraging early steps in a long journey in Europe-China talks. That
is better than nothing, but hardly enough to expect progress within a
reasonable timeframe. Meanwhile, the risks of a disorderly unwind will
keep rising.
TWO ZONES OF CONCERN IN FOCUS: FRANCE AND THE US

France was a top concern for many participants. They feared a repeat
of the 2010–2012 Eurozone debt crisis, seeing a country with public
debt approaching 120% of GDP , sub-par growth, a poor record of fiscal
consolidation, riots in the streets and presidential elections six mon -
ths away, with populist candidates leading the polls. French and Eu -
ropean policymakers, alongside private-sector participants, presented
a different picture, namely: a fiscal situation that is serious, but is

being managed (the draft budget provides for meaningful adjustment
and is very likely to be adopted without being weakened); a widening
consensus from centre-left to far-right which favours a return to fiscal
responsibility, in line with a similar shift in public opinion; and the
electoral process is only beginning, with the outcome still wide open —
not least because the candidates’ names and numbers are not known.

### Page 2

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
It was also pointed out that the quantum of fiscal adjustment required
is not out of line with previous consolidation episodes in France in the
2010s and the 1990s. Still, most participants left not much reassured
and expecting a bumpy ride ahead.
US participants outside government saw a growing likelihood of a De -
mocratic sweep in the midterm elections, Either way, they expected

little fiscal consolidation, despite a manifestly challenging fiscal path
that may end up causing volatility in financial markets. Non-US parti -
cipants also feared growing policy unpredictability.
OVERSHADOWED TOPICS: THE STARS OF RECENT IMF MEETINGS
Tariffs were entirely absent from the agenda and conversations. Toke-
nised finance (rather than the narrower topic of stablecoins) still fea -
tured, but far less prominently: as the field takes shape, discussion

has shifted from the conceptual what and why to the technical how.
Climate change and low-income countries remained on the agenda,
but far less in focus than at previous Meetings. The sheer number of
issues demanding attention may be overwhelming financial policyma-
kers’ bandwidth.
*****
All in all, while the global context remains extraordinarily challenging,
it’s comforting to see that global financial policy makers are broadly
alert to both risks and opportunities, and to what needs to be done for

the latter to prevail. Now they need to deliver.
Isabelle Mateos y Lago
isabelle.mateosylago@bnpparibas.com

SUMMARY: AI euphoria meets macro headwinds
Tech remained a major driver of equity market performance in Q3. The
Mag 7 outperformed the broader market while the S&P 500 also

posted gains, lifted by mega-cap and tech stocks.
Still, tech’s path in Q3 was volatile. July began with a reversal of the AI
trade as investors questioned elevated valuations, the sustainability of
AI spending and the outlook for semiconductor demand. That led to a
large sell-off in chip stocks, with large falls in Q2 outperformers SK
Hynix and Samsung contributing to a sharp decline in Korea’s KOSPI.
The Philly SOX experienced its worst monthly performance since

2008.
By August, however, sentiment had improved. The S&P 500 reached
fresh record highs, even as US Treasury yields rose, supported by
stronger cloud earnings and signs of AI monetization. September
proved more challenging as higher oil prices, inflation concerns and
rising bond yields weighed on risk assets, particularly semiconductors.
The Fed also raised its policy rate by 25bp on Sep 16.
Yet, despite these macro headwinds, company guidance continued to

indicate strong AI infrastructure demand, with Micron’s stronger-than-
expected revenue guidance on Sep 30 providing further evidence of
robust demand for memory chips. Memory supply remains tight,
semiconductor capex is accelerating, and the industry’s attention has
increasingly turned to bottlenecks in optical networking and photonics,
as well as concerns around AI regulation and safety. Looking ahead,
tech is likely to remain an important driver of equity returns into Q4,

although elevated valuations and tighter financial conditions leave the
sector sensitive to earnings disappointments. Sources: Deutsche Bank Research, Bloomberg Finance LP. Updated Sep 30. Returns in local currency.

1. GLOBAL INDUSTRY BREAKDOWN
Tech hardware, including Lenovo, Advantech, and HP Inc, rebounded in Q3. This was partly driven by accelerating AI

server demand and the ongoing data center build-out, although continuing memory shortages raised costs for
hardware manufacturers. Cloud and server providers, like Oracle, Dell and HPE, also highlighted strong AI-related
demand. Semis and equipment were the weakest performers. Elsewhere, entertainment and telecoms posted modest
declines. Finally, in Q3 investor attention increasingly shifted to AI agents, with Meta’s Muse, Microsoft’s Copilot and

Salesforce’s Agentforce among the prominent offerings.
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Bloomberg Finance LP. Median returns taken. Updated Sep 30.
